I end up putting them off more than I should. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about these tasks or try to start them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, I don’t feel panicky or anything like that. It’s more like… I know I should be doing it, and I get this uncomfortable feeling because I’m not starting. Maybe some pressure, I guess. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding these tasks or putting them off until the last minute?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to, and I don’t want it to sound like I’m just being careless, but the tasks that need a lot of sitting down and thinking—like the monthly reports or planning a health event across different sites—those are the ones I tend to put off. It’s like I know they matter, and I know I need to do them, but when I go to start, I kind of hit a wall. I end up putting them off more than I should. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about these tasks or try to start them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, I don’t feel panicky or anything like that. It’s more like… I know I should be doing it, and I get this uncomfortable feeling because I’m not starting. Maybe some pressure, I guess. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding these tasks or putting them off until the last minute?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to, and I don’t want it to sound like I’m just being careless, but the tasks that need a lot of sitting down and thinking—like the monthly reports or planning a health event across different sites—those are the ones I tend to put off. It’s like I know they matter, and I know I need to do them, but when I go to start, I kind of hit a wall. Then I end up waiting longer than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’m someone who worries all the time, at least not in a big way. I mean, I think about work and responsibilities like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worried day to day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m restless in a really obvious way. I’m not pacing around or anything. Maybe I shift in my chair sometimes or tap a pen without thinking, but I haven’t really paid close attention to it. It doesn’t feel like the main thing to me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future or past?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I think about what’s coming up or maybe replay something if it didn’t go well, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck in the past or future a lot. At least, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing. I try to look like I’m following along, but sometimes I have to piece together what was said afterward. Does that count as what you mean by difficulty sustaining focus?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I can manage a lot of day-to-day stuff, but when it’s something that needs real sitting-down thinking—like monthly reports or planning a bigger multi-site health event—I just sort of hit a wall.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks for seeing me. I guess the main thing is… I’ve been having a hard time with tasks that require a lot of sit-down thinking. Like monthly reports, or planning a health event across multiple sites—things I know are important and I can do, but when I try to start, it feels like I hit a wall. I end up putting them off more than I should. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about these tasks or try to start them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, I don’t feel panicky or anything like that. It’s more like… I know I should be doing it, and I get this uncomfortable feeling because I’m not starting. Maybe some pressure, I guess. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding these tasks or putting them off until the last minute?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to, and I don’t want it to sound like I’m just being careless, but the tasks that need a lot of sitting down and thinking—like the monthly reports or planning a health event across different sites—those are the ones I tend to put off. It’s like I know they matter, and I know I need to do them, but when I go to start, I kind of hit a wall. Then I end up waiting longer than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’m someone who worries all the time, at least not in a big way. I mean, I think about work and responsibilities like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worried day to day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m restless in a really obvious way. I’m not pacing around or anything. Maybe I shift in my chair sometimes or tap a pen without thinking, but I haven’t really paid close attention to it. It doesn’t feel like the main thing to me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future or past?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I think about what’s coming up or maybe replay something if it didn’t go well, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck in the past or future a lot. At least, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not usually. I mean, I try to think things through, especially with work or family stuff. Maybe I’ll make a quick decision here and there like anyone does, but I wouldn’t say I’m acting impulsively a lot or doing things without considering them. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one does happen. I don’t want to overstate it, because I can focus when I really need to, but in meetings especially, like if someone’s going over a budget update, I’ll be trying to listen and then a phone vibrates, or people are talking out in the hall, or even just a thought pops in about something I forgot to do for the league, and then I realize I’ve missed the next few minutes.\n\nIt’s a little embarrassing, honestly. I try to look like I’m following along, but sometimes I have to piece together what was said afterward. Does that count as what you mean by difficulty sustaining focus?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have off days like anybody, and I can get frustrated or worn out, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the time. At least, that’s not how I’d describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have persistent feelings of worry that are hard to control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I worry about normal things—work, keeping up with responsibilities, family stuff—but it doesn’t feel like this constant worry I can’t control. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way. If something specific is going on, sure, I’ll think about it, but I don’t feel worried most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… probably more than I’d like to admit. In meetings especially, I’ll be trying to pay attention to something like a budget update, and then a phone vibrates or people are talking out in the hallway, and I’m just… gone for a bit.\n\nSometimes it’s not even something outside me—it’ll be a random thought, like something I forgot to handle for the league, and then I realize I missed the next few minutes. I do try to refocus, and I don’t mean to seem careless or anything. But yes, I guess I do get pulled away pretty easily. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, like you need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can shift in my chair or get up for coffee if I’ve been sitting a long time, but I don’t feel like I have this strong need to move around all the time. I wouldn’t say physical restlessness is a big issue for me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your thoughts or tasks to get started on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen. I try to keep things organized, and I don’t want it to sound like I’m just letting things fall apart, but when I’m juggling work projects, the memorial committee stuff, and outreach events, it can get messy.\n\nI’ll have notes in, like, three different places, and then I kind of freeze on what needs to happen first. Then I spend too much time trying to sort it out, and before I know it I’m rushing at the end to get everything done. So yes, I guess getting started and organizing the order of things can be a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say “often.” I mean, I feel bad when I drop the ball on something or realize I should’ve followed up sooner, sure. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.0, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks for seeing me. I guess the main thing is… I’ve been having a hard time with tasks that require a lot of sit-down thinking. Like monthly reports, or planning a health event across multiple sites—things I know are important and I can do, but when I try to start, it feels like I hit a wall. I end up putting them off more than I should. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about these tasks or try to start them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, I don’t feel panicky or anything like that. It’s more like… I know I should be doing it, and I get this uncomfortable feeling because I’m not starting. Maybe some pressure, I guess. But I don’t know if that’s anxiety in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding these tasks or putting them off until the last minute?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to, and I don’t want it to sound like I’m just being careless, but the tasks that need a lot of sitting down and thinking—like the monthly reports or planning a health event across different sites—those are the ones I tend to put off. It’s like I know they matter, and I know I need to do them, but when I go to start, I kind of hit a wall. Then I end up waiting longer than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I’m someone who worries all the time, at least not in a big way. I mean, I think about work and responsibilities like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worried day to day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m restless in a really obvious way. I’m not pacing around or anything. Maybe I shift in my chair sometimes or tap a pen without thinking, but I haven’t really paid close attention to it. It doesn’t feel like the main thing to me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future or past?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I think about what’s coming up or maybe replay something if it didn’t go well, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck in the past or future a lot. At least, I haven’t really noticed that as a major thing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not usually. I mean, I try to think things through, especially with work or family stuff. Maybe I’ll make a quick decision here and there like anyone does, but I wouldn’t say I’m acting impulsively a lot or doing things without considering them. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one does happen. I don’t want to overstate it, because I can focus when I really need to, but in meetings especially, like if someone’s going over a budget update, I’ll be trying to listen and then a phone vibrates, or people are talking out in the hall, or even just a thought pops in about something I forgot to do for the league, and then I realize I’ve missed the next few minutes.\n\nIt’s a little embarrassing, honestly. I try to look like I’m following along, but sometimes I have to piece together what was said afterward. Does that count as what you mean by difficulty sustaining focus?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have off days like anybody, and I can get frustrated or worn out, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the time. At least, that’s not how I’d describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have persistent feelings of worry that are hard to control?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I worry about normal things—work, keeping up with responsibilities, family stuff—but it doesn’t feel like this constant worry I can’t control. At least, I wouldn’t describe it that way. If something specific is going on, sure, I’ll think about it, but I don’t feel worried most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… probably more than I’d like to admit. In meetings especially, I’ll be trying to pay attention to something like a budget update, and then a phone vibrates or people are talking out in the hallway, and I’m just… gone for a bit.\n\nSometimes it’s not even something outside me—it’ll be a random thought, like something I forgot to handle for the league, and then I realize I missed the next few minutes. I do try to refocus, and I don’t mean to seem careless or anything. But yes, I guess I do get pulled away pretty easily. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, like you need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can shift in my chair or get up for coffee if I’ve been sitting a long time, but I don’t feel like I have this strong need to move around all the time. I wouldn’t say physical restlessness is a big issue for me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your thoughts or tasks to get started on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen. I try to keep things organized, and I don’t want it to sound like I’m just letting things fall apart, but when I’m juggling work projects, the memorial committee stuff, and outreach events, it can get messy.\n\nI’ll have notes in, like, three different places, and then I kind of freeze on what needs to happen first. Then I spend too much time trying to sort it out, and before I know it I’m rushing at the end to get everything done. So yes, I guess getting started and organizing the order of things can be a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say “often.” I mean, I feel bad when I drop the ball on something or realize I should’ve followed up sooner, sure. But I try not to dwell on it too much, or at least I tell myself that.\n\nI guess I do sometimes replay things in my head and think, “I should’ve handled that better,” but I’m not sure if that’s guilt in the way you mean. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or moving around more than other people?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. If something specific is going on, sure, I’ll think about it, but I don’t feel worried most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… probably more than I’d like to admit. In meetings especially, I’ll be trying to pay attention to something like a budget update, and then a phone vibrates or people are talking out in the hallway, and I’m just… gone for a bit.\n\nSometimes it’s not even something outside me—it’ll be a random thought, like something I forgot to handle for the league, and then I realize I missed the next few minutes. I do try to refocus, and I don’t mean to seem careless or anything. But yes, I guess I do get pulled away pretty easily. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, like you need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can shift in my chair or get up for coffee if I’ve been sitting a long time, but I don’t feel like I have this strong need to move around all the time. I wouldn’t say physical restlessness is a big issue for me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your thoughts or tasks to get started on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen. I try to keep things organized, and I don’t want it to sound like I’m just letting things fall apart, but when I’m juggling work projects, the memorial committee stuff, and outreach events, it can get messy.\n\nI’ll have notes in, like, three different places, and then I kind of freeze on what needs to happen first. Then I spend too much time trying to sort it out, and before I know it I’m rushing at the end to get everything done. So yes, I guess getting started and organizing the order of things can be a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say “often.” I mean, I feel bad when I drop the ball on something or realize I should’ve followed up sooner, sure. But I try not to dwell on it too much, or at least I tell myself that.\n\nI guess I do sometimes replay things in my head and think, “I should’ve handled that better,” but I’m not sure if that’s guilt in the way you mean. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or moving around more than other people?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I might tap a pen or adjust how I’m sitting, but I don’t think it’s more than other people. At least nobody’s really pointed it out to me. So I’d say not really, unless there’s something specific you’re thinking of?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself engaging in excessive movement, like pacing or fidgeting, when you're not actively doing something that requires it?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, if I’m waiting on something I might shift around or maybe tap my fingers a little, but I wouldn’t say I’m pacing or moving excessively. I don’t think that’s a big issue for me, at least not that I’ve noticed. Does that seem like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to focus when you're working on tasks that need a lot of thinking or mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I can manage a lot of day-to-day stuff, but when it’s something that needs real sitting-down thinking—like monthly reports or planning a bigger multi-site health event—I just sort of hit a wall.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m not trying, because I do know it matters, but I’ll keep putting it off and telling myself I’ll do it when I can focus better. And then it’s still sitting there. So yes, that kind of task is definitely harder for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you're working on tasks that require attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to be careful, so I don’t want to overstate it. But yes, it does happen more than I’d like.\n\nLike I’ll send out a clinic staffing schedule or an email for the soccer league thinking I’ve checked it, and then someone will point out I had the wrong date in there, or I left off the location, or missed some small detail. And it’s frustrating because it’s usually something I feel like I should’ve caught before sending it. So… yes, I guess that would count.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with tasks that require a lot of thinking is more related to having trouble focusing or to feeling overwhelmed by the task itself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably both, but if I’m being honest, it feels more like I can’t get my focus to stay with it once I sit down. Like with monthly reports or planning one of those bigger multi-site events, I know it’s important, and I’m not trying to avoid responsibility or anything… but it just feels like I hit a wall.\n\nThen I’ll put it off and tell myself I’ll do it when my head is clearer, and somehow that keeps happening. So, yes, maybe the task feels overwhelming, but I think the bigger issue is that it takes sustained brain work and I have a hard time staying locked in on it. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
